be subtler than their products, each display internal pattern, each possess productive power, and
each differ in stage from its effects. None of the four takes unity as its premise, so none can
deliver unity as its conclusion.

The fifth argument alone does. It begins from the observation that cosmic diversity is not a heap
but one interconnected whole, and reasons that a diversified yet undivided cosmos points back to an
original source in which later differentiations are not yet separated. Admitting many disconnected
primal materials would therefore destroy the very undividedness from which the inference proceeds.

Justification, stated as a bridge: the premise of proof five is the world's undivided
interconnectedness, so plurality at the root would falsify the premise and not merely complicate the
conclusion.

The verdict holds within Sāṃkhya's own inferential licence; a Nyāya atomist declines it by tracing
interconnectedness to coordination among many causes rather than to a single origin.

---

<a id="formal_session-226-2020-q7-a-20-marks-e6181b983b"></a>
#### 2020 · Q7(a) · 20 marks

> **Printed question.** “A mango tree is grown out of a mango seed.” How will Sāṃkhya system explain
> this process through their theory of causation by rejecting their rival perspectives?

**Demand decoded.** Two obligations. Explain the example through the doctrine of the pre-existent
effect *and* reject the rivals by name. An answer without the rival-rejection half cannot score.

**Model answer (357 words).**

Sāṃkhya explains the example by the doctrine that the effect pre-exists in its material cause
(**satkāryavāda**), in its specific form as real transformation (**pariṇāmavāda**). The mango tree
pre-exists in the mango seed not as a miniature gross tree spatially concealed within it, but as a
latent determinate capacity in a material continuum; production is manifestation (**abhivyakti**) of
what is latent, and the effect differs from the cause by state (**avasthā**), not by substance.

Five arguments support this. Because a non-entity cannot be produced (**asadakaraṇāt**), no
operation yields oil from sand. Because a determinate material cause is taken up
(**upādāna-grahaṇāt**), the cultivator selects a mango seed and not sand. Because everything cannot
arise from everything (**sarvasambhavābhāvāt**), and non-existence is featureless and therefore
indiscriminable, sheer prior absence cannot explain why this seed yields this tree. Because the
potent produces only the producible (**śaktasya śakya-karaṇāt**), a potency directed at a determinate
effect presupposes that effect as its object. Because the effect has the being of the cause
(**kāraṇa-bhāvāt**), the tree continues the seed's material substance as cloth continues threads.

The rivals must now be rejected. Nyāya-Vaiśeṣika holds that the effect is absent beforehand as prior
non-existence (**prāgabhāva**) and that production is a new beginning; it objects that Sāṃkhya
equivocates between the absolutely non-existent and the merely not-yet-existent, and that the whole
(**avayavin**) is a new substance with new properties. Sāṃkhya replies that a not-yet-existent effect
must still be something determinate in the cause, else the operation has no terminus, and that new
properties are new arrangements (**saṃsthāna**), not new substances from non-being. Advaita agrees
that being does not arise from non-being but makes the transformation apparent
(**vivartavāda**); Sāṃkhya replies that an appearance-theory cannot explain why production is
selective. Buddhism explains becoming by dependent origination without an enduring material root;
Sāṃkhya replies that momentary series cannot ground the observed material continuity of seed and
tree.

The objection that production becomes pointless is answered by manifestation: effort removes
obstruction and supplies conditions rather than creating being.

The doctrine is therefore strongest as an argument about determinacy, and remains contestable only
where an opponent grounds determinacy in relational potency instead of latent effect.

---

<a id="formal_session-227-2021-q6-b-15-marks-bfce78470e"></a>
#### 2021 · Q6(b) · 15 marks

> **Printed question.** Explain the Sāṃkhya view on three guṇas (guṇa-traya) and their
> modifications.

**Demand decoded.** Two halves of equal weight. The triad is the first half; the two kinds of
modification are the second, and omitting them forfeits roughly half the marks.

**Model answer (250 words).**

The three qualities (**guṇa**) are luminosity (**sattva**), activity (**rajas**) and inertia
(**tamas**). They are not three separable substances but three inseparable constitutive tendencies
of one primordial nature, present together in every evolute in varying proportion.

Luminosity is light, clear and buoyant; its affective tone is pleasure and its function is
revelation, clarity and manifestation. Activity is dynamic, restless and projective; its tone is
pain and its function is energy, stimulation and movement. Inertia is heavy, obstructive and
resistant; its tone is delusion and its function is concealment, resistance and material density.
Because character, affective tone and evolutionary function are mapped together, a single triad
explains physical constitution, mental state and moral temperament at once — Sāṃkhya's most
economical achievement.

Their modification (**guṇa-pariṇāma**) takes two forms. In equilibrium (**sāmyāvasthā**) the three
stand in exact balance and nature is unmanifest (**avyakta**); here each quality transforms within
its own nature, which is homogeneous modification (**sarūpa-pariṇāma**). This preserves the claim
that nature is never inert, so that dissolution is not annihilation. When equilibrium is disturbed
in the presence of the conscious witness, the qualities enter asymmetrical relations and generate
differentiated evolutes: this is heterogeneous modification (**virūpa-pariṇāma**), to which actual
cosmological evolution belongs.

The disturbance is not a mechanical collision. The witness is inactive, so Sāṃkhya describes only
functional activation in proximity.

The doctrine is therefore powerful as an account of qualitative variety and weakest exactly at its
trigger: what converts homogeneous into heterogeneous modification is presence rather than
causation, and Śaṃkara presses precisely this point.

---

<a id="formal_session-228-2022-q5-a-10-marks-d88128edc9"></a>
#### 2022 · Q5(a) · 10 marks

> **Printed question.** Examine and evaluate the proofs given by Sāṃkhya philosophy to prove the
> existence of Puruṣa.

**Demand decoded.** Two directives. "Examine" wants the internal logic of the proofs; "evaluate"
wants a reasoned judgement on their force. Listing alone answers neither.

**Model answer (194 words).**

Sāṃkhya offers five arguments, all sharing one premise: the entire objective complex is unconscious,
instrumental and presented to experience.

Aggregates exist for another (**saṅghāta-parārthatvāt**): body, senses, mind and intellect are
complexes, and a complex functions instrumentally for a user distinct from it. The witness must be
other than the qualities (**triguṇādi-viparyayāt**): the knower of pleasure, pain and delusion cannot
be the same changing states that are known. Organised experience requires a presiding locus
(**adhiṣṭhānāt**) — an inference to a conscious witness of lived order, and expressly not a proof of
God. Experience requires an enjoyer (**bhoktṛ-bhāvāt**), since presented states demand a subject for
whom they appear. Striving for release presupposes a liberable witness (**kaivalyārthaṃ
pravṛtteḥ**).

Evaluating them, their force is cumulative rather than individual. Each alone invites a deflationary
reply — a Buddhist explains coordination by causal series and ownership by convention — but together
they converge on the structural point that a domain of presentation without a subject-pole is not a
domain of presentation at all. Their form throughout is general-correlation inference.

Their honest limit is that they establish a subject-pole, not yet an eternal, plural and wholly
inactive one.

---

<a id="formal_session-229-2023-q5-b-10-marks-8ab8d04b02"></a>
#### 2023 · Q5(b) · 10 marks

> **Printed question.** “If Puruṣa and Prakṛti are two completely independent realities, then no
> relation between the two is possible.” In the light of this statement make a brief presentation of
> Śaṅkara’s criticism of Sāṃkhya dualism.

**Demand decoded.** "Brief presentation … in the light of this statement" means the quoted relation
problem must organise the answer, and the criticism must be Śaṃkara's, not a general objection.

**Model answer (190 words).**

The quoted statement isolates Sāṃkhya's contact problem: if the conscious witness is wholly inactive
and primordial nature wholly unconscious, no intelligible real relation appears possible, yet
evolution, bondage and liberation all presuppose one.

Sāṃkhya answers that the relation is mere proximity (**sānnidhya**), not gross contact: nature
becomes active in the witness's mere presence. Two analogies support this — a magnet moves iron
without deliberative willing, and a clear crystal appears coloured by a nearby flower without itself
changing. The first explains activation by presence; the second explains apparent contamination
without real change.

Śaṃkara attacks both. Even a magnet exerts force, so if the witness were genuinely like a magnet it
would not be wholly inactive. The crystal may explain appearance, but it cannot explain the
initiation of the world-process, since a crystal initiates nothing. He adds that an unconscious chief
principle cannot be the Upaniṣadic cause, which is described as conscious and knowing, and that
purposive evolution "for the sake of" the witness presupposes intelligence.

His conclusion is that the dualism is unstable: it must either collapse toward monism or admit a
third mediating principle — a demand for further explanation rather than a refutation by assertion.

---

<a id="formal_session-230-2024-q7-a-20-marks-10-10-46a76807b5"></a>
#### 2024 · Q7(a) · 20 marks (10+10)

> **Printed question.** Present an account of evolution of Prakṛti as propounded in Sāṃkhyakārikā.
> In this context, also explain the difference between buddhi, mahat and ahaṃkāra.

**Demand decoded.** An explicitly split question. The first ten marks are the complete evolutionary
sequence; the second ten are the three-term distinction, whose correct answer is that two of the
three terms name one principle.

**Model answer (382 words).**

**Part one: the evolution of primordial nature.** Primordial nature (**prakṛti**) is the unmanifest
chief principle (**avyakta pradhāna**), one, unconscious and uncreated, constituted by the three
qualities in equilibrium. In the mere presence of the conscious witness the equilibrium is disturbed,
and heterogeneous modification of the qualities (**virūpa-pariṇāma**) begins.

The first evolute is the great one, also called the determinative intellect (**mahat / buddhi**).
From it arises the I-maker (**ahaṃkāra**), the principle of individuation. The I-maker then evolves
along two lines. From its luminous aspect arise the coordinating mind (**manas**), the five cognitive
capacities — hearing, touch, sight, taste and smell — and the five action capacities: speech,
grasping, locomotion, excretion and reproduction. From its inert aspect arise the five subtle
elements (**tanmātra**), the potentials of sound, touch, form, taste and smell; and from these arise
the five gross elements (**mahābhūta**) — ether, air, fire, water and earth — on the standard
correspondence of sound to ether, touch to air, form to fire, taste to water and smell to earth. The
active quality supplies energy to both lines and produces no separate set of principles.

Twenty-four evolutes plus the conscious witness give twenty-five principles. The fourfold
classification controls the list: nature alone is cause but not effect; the intellect, the I-maker
and the five subtle elements are both cause and effect; the mind, the ten capacities and the five
gross elements are effects only; and the witness is neither.

**Part two: buddhi, mahat and ahaṃkāra.** The decisive point is that the great one and the
determinative intellect are **the same principle** under two names: the great one is its cosmic
aspect, the determinative intellect its individual or psychological aspect. Writing them as two
successive stages is an error that also corrupts the total count.

The I-maker is genuinely distinct and is derived *from* that first evolute. Functionally, the
determinative intellect makes the final ascertainment (**adhyavasāya**) — judgement and
discrimination — and, being predominantly luminous and transparent, receives the reflection of the
witness. The I-maker appropriates functions as "I" and "mine", and false selfhood crystallises there,
which is why it is central to bondage. The coordinating mind, arising later, only synthesises
sense-data.

The sequence therefore unifies cosmology and psychology: the equipment of knowing is itself a product
of the known.

---

<a id="formal_session-231-2025-q7-a-20-marks-e138e91488"></a>
#### 2025 · Q7(a) · 20 marks

> **Printed question.** Why does Śaṅkara consider Sāṃkhya Philosophy as his chief opponent (pradhāna
> malla)? Examine his arguments against Sāṃkhya Philosophy.

**Demand decoded.** Two halves again. "Why … chief opponent" asks for the grounds of the
designation; "examine his arguments" asks for the objections tested for force. Answers that supply
only the objections lose the first half entirely.

**Model answer (374 words).**

**Why chief.** The title of chief wrestler (**pradhāna malla**) records systematic strength, not
contempt. Three grounds support it.

First, Sāṃkhya is the most sophisticated non-Vedāntic rival at the level of ultimate explanation: it
accounts for world, self, bondage, liberation and causation within one coherent metaphysical system,
and is therefore near enough to require detailed refutation rather than dismissal. Second, its
doctrine of real transformation (**pariṇāmavāda**) is the nearest rival to Advaita's apparent
transformation (**vivartavāda**); both deny production from non-being and both derive the world from
an ultimate, differing only on whether the derivation is real. Third, both appeal to a single
ultimate explanatory ground — Sāṃkhya to one unconscious primal material principle, Advaita to
conscious Brahman — so the contest is direct rather than oblique.

**The arguments, examined.** Śaṃkara's first objection is that an unconscious chief principle cannot
be the Upaniṣadic cause, since the scriptural source is described as conscious and knowing;
"knowing-cause" descriptions exclude an insentient nature. This is powerful within a scripturally
governed enquiry, though a non-Vedic opponent may refuse the premise.

His second objection is the contact problem: an inactive witness and an unconscious nature cannot
intelligibly relate, yet the whole system depends on their relation. Sāṃkhya answers with mere
proximity, supported by the magnet moving iron and the crystal coloured by a nearby flower. Śaṃkara
replies that a magnet exerts force — so the analogy either fails or destroys the witness's
inactivity — and that the crystal explains appearance but not the initiation of the world-process.
This objection is the strongest, because the two analogies do different jobs and neither does both.

His third objection is teleological: if nature evolves "for the sake of" the witness, purposiveness
appears to require intelligence. Sāṃkhya replies that purposive functioning need not be conscious
planning, as milk flows for the calf; the reply preserves internal sufficiency but does not remove
the pressure.

**Verdict.** Śaṃkara treats Sāṃkhya as chief opponent because it attempts, from an unconscious first
principle, exactly what Advaita attempts from a conscious one. He rejects it because the ultimate
cause must be conscious, teleology cannot rest on unconscious nature, and the witness–nature relation
remains unstable — a demonstration that the dualism needs further explanation rather than a proof
that Advaita is thereby established.

---

<a id="formal_session-232-2021-q5-a-10-marks-routed-b3b3a085be"></a>
#### 2021 · Q5(a) · 10 marks · routed

> **Printed question.** Does the seed contain the tree? Discuss with reference to Nyāya-Vaiśeṣika
> Philosophy.

**Routing note.** Primary ownership lies with Nyāya-Vaiśeṣika, but the question is unanswerable
without the Sāṃkhya position it is framed against, and the Sāṃkhya side must be stated accurately.

**Model answer (188 words).**

For Nyāya-Vaiśeṣika the answer is no. Before production the effect is characterised by prior
non-existence (**prāgabhāva**) in its material cause; production is a genuinely new beginning
(**ārambhavāda**), and the resulting whole (**avayavin**) is a new substance inhering in its parts
and possessing properties the parts lack — a cloth covers, whereas separate threads do not.

The question is framed against Sāṃkhya, which answers yes on its own terms: the effect pre-exists as
a latent determinate capacity, since non-existence is featureless and cannot explain why this seed
yields this tree, and since a potency directed at a determinate effect presupposes that effect.
Sāṃkhya does not claim that a miniature gross tree is spatially hidden in the seed.

Nyāya's reply is that Sāṃkhya equivocates: it denies only that the tree exists *as* the tree
beforehand, not that the seed is determinate. Determinacy is secured instead by the causal capacity
of the material and by the exclusion of non-causal antecedents.

The dispute therefore turns on whether determinacy can be carried by a relational potency alone, or
must be grounded in a latent effect — a difference of first principles rather than of evidence.

---

<a id="formal_session-233-2022-q5-e-10-marks-routed-6b9ebd92ab"></a>
#### 2022 · Q5(e) · 10 marks · routed

> **Printed question.** Do you agree with the view that ‘Vivartavāda is the logical development of
> Pariṇāmavāda’? Give reasons in support of your answer.

**Routing note.** Owned by Vedānta, but the question is a direct examination of whether Sāṃkhya's
real transformation entails Advaita's apparent transformation.

**Model answer (181 words).**

I agree only partly, and the qualification carries the argument.

The claim is defensible in one respect. Both doctrines deny production from sheer non-being, so both
belong to a broad pre-existence orientation; and apparent transformation can be presented as the
result of pressing real transformation's own logic — if the ultimate must not be diminished by
producing, transformation is best read as appearance rather than as real change.

It is indefensible in another. Real transformation is not an incomplete version of apparent
transformation but a rival with opposite commitments. Sāṃkhya's cause is unconscious primordial
nature and its world is a real evolute; Advaita's cause is conscious Brahman and its world is
ultimately appearance. Sāṃkhya's mechanism is the modification of the three qualities; Advaita's is
superimposition. Treating one as the logical development of the other therefore assumes the very
Vedāntic premise — that an ultimate cannot really change — which Sāṃkhya explicitly denies by
defining nature as that whose being is modification.

The view is thus historically suggestive as a description of Vedāntic self-understanding, but
logically question-begging as a claim about entailment.

---

<a id="formal_session-234-2023-q8-b-15-marks-routed-292fbdda45"></a>
#### 2023 · Q8(b) · 15 marks · routed

> **Printed question.** Write a note on Nyāya notion of Prāgabhāva (prior non-existence). How does
> this notion help Naiyāyikas in defending their position on causation against the Sāṃkhya view of
> causation? Critically discuss.

**Routing note.** Owned by Nyāya-Vaiśeṣika; the second and third clauses make Sāṃkhya's causal
doctrine the subject under test, so the Sāṃkhya side must be argued and not merely named.

**Model answer (296 words).**

Prior non-existence (**prāgabhāva**) is one of the four absences recognised in the Vaiśeṣika
category-scheme. It is the absence of an effect in its material cause before production: beginningless
but with an end, since it terminates precisely when the effect is produced. Every absence has a
counter-positive, and here the counter-positive is the effect itself.

The notion does exact defensive work for Nyāya's doctrine that the effect does not pre-exist
(**asatkāryavāda**). It gives a positive account of the pre-production situation, so production
becomes the destruction of prior non-existence rather than creation from nothing; it preserves the
reality of production, since something genuinely new begins; and it blocks the charge of
arbitrariness, because determinacy is located in the causal capacity of the material and in the
exclusion of non-causal antecedents rather than in a latent effect.

Against Sāṃkhya it grounds a dilemma. If the effect exists beforehand, the causal operation is idle;
if it does not exist as the effect, Nyāya's position has been conceded, and prior non-existence names
exactly what the effect then lacks. Nyāya adds that the whole is a new substance with properties its
parts lack.

Sāṃkhya replies that a merely negative antecedent cannot discriminate outcomes, since non-existence
is featureless; that production is manifestation (**abhivyakti**) and effort removes obstruction; and
that cause and effect differ by state (**avasthā**) rather than by substance, so the operation is not
idle.

Critically, prior non-existence answers the charge of creation from nothing but relocates rather than
removes the demand for determinacy, which Nyāya must then discharge through causal capacity — the
very notion Sāṃkhya claims is unintelligible without a latent effect.

---

<a id="formal_session-235-original-solved-mains-practice-4e170a601d"></a>
### ORIGINAL SOLVED MAINS PRACTICE

> Six original questions written in previous-year style: two at ten marks, two at fifteen and two at
> twenty. They are learner practice, not previous-year questions.

<a id="formal_session-236-original-1-10-marks-659467fd32"></a>
#### Original 1 · 10 marks

> **Question.** Explain why Sāṃkhya restricts the means of valid knowledge to three, and show how
> this restriction shapes its metaphysics.

**Model answer (177 words).**

Sāṃkhya admits exactly three instruments of knowledge — perception, inference and reliable testimony
— and reduces comparison, postulation, non-apprehension, inclusion and traditional report to these
three rather than rejecting them as worthless. Comparison operates through a prior verbal report
applied inferentially; postulation is inference to the best explanatory condition; non-apprehension
belongs to the failure-conditions of perception.

This restriction is not economy for its own sake; it creates an obligation. Both ultimates —
primordial nature and the conscious witness — are beyond the senses (**atīndriya**), so perception
cannot reach them, and testimony alone would beg the question against a non-Vedic opponent.
Imperceptibility itself is disarmed by the standard list of causes of non-perception, in which root
nature falls under subtlety (**saukṣmya**).

The whole evidential weight therefore falls on general-correlation inference
(**sāmānyato-dṛṣṭa**), which alone can reach a term whose invariable relation was never observed
directly. The five proofs of nature and the five proofs of the witness are precisely the shape this
epistemology forces.

The metaphysics is thus epistemically driven — and correspondingly vulnerable if that inferential
licence is denied, as Cārvāka denies it.

---

<a id="formal_session-237-original-2-10-marks-7e47f5b00c"></a>
#### Original 2 · 10 marks

> **Question.** "Nothing happens to Puruṣa at liberation." Comment on this statement with reference
> to the Sāṃkhya account of bondage and kaivalya.

**Model answer (180 words).**

The statement is accurate in strict Sāṃkhya and is the best available formulation of its
soteriology.

Bondage is non-discrimination (**aviveka**) between the witness and nature. The determinative
intellect, being transparent, receives the reflection of consciousness, and false appropriation
(**abhimāna**) follows — "I am the doer, knower, enjoyer". Since this is misattribution and not
modification, the witness is not really altered by bondage.

Liberation is correspondingly cognitive. Discriminative knowledge (**viveka-jñāna**) stabilises the
distinction between witness-consciousness and quality-evolutes, false identification ends, and the
resulting state is isolation (**kaivalya**) — disentanglement, not merger and not annihilation. The
canonical image is the dancer who retires once she has been seen; the potter's wheel explains why the
body continues by prior momentum, giving liberation while living (**jīvanmukti**) before final
release (**videhamukti**).

The strict formulation follows: the witness is never really bound, liberated or transmigrating, since
these belong to nature and the subtle body.

The comment must end with the cost. If nothing happens to the witness, the school must explain in
what sense the problem was ever *its* problem — a clarification it offers but does not demonstrate.

---

<a id="formal_session-238-original-3-15-marks-5919c9fc97"></a>
#### Original 3 · 15 marks

> **Question.** Examine the claim that Sāṃkhya's theory of the three qualities makes cosmology and
> psychology a single subject.

**Model answer (270 words).**

The claim is substantially correct and identifies Sāṃkhya's most economical achievement, though it
must be stated with care.

The three qualities are luminosity, activity and inertia: not three substances but three inseparable
constitutive tendencies of one nature, present together in every evolute in varying proportion. Each
is defined simultaneously along three registers — character, affective tone and evolutionary function.
Luminosity is buoyant, carries pleasure and performs manifestation; activity is restless, carries
pain and performs movement; inertia is heavy, carries delusion and performs concealment.

Because a single triad is defined across physical, affective and functional registers at once, the
same explanatory apparatus accounts for the density of a stone, the dullness of a mind and the
confusion of a temperament. The evolutionary sequence completes the unification: intellect, I-maker,
mind and the ten capacities are evolutes of the same nature that yields the subtle and gross elements,
so the equipment of knowing is itself a product of the known.

Two qualifications are needed. First, the unification is achieved by placing all mental functioning
inside unconscious nature, which is why Sāṃkhya is a consciousness–nature dualism and not a mind–body
dualism; the psychology it unifies with cosmology is a psychology *without* consciousness. Second, the
unification does not extend to the witness, which is beyond the qualities altogether, so exactly one
thing remains outside the single subject.

Examined, then, the claim holds for everything the qualities constitute and fails precisely at the
point where Sāṃkhya's dualism begins. That is not a defect in the theory of the qualities but the
boundary the theory was designed to respect — and it is also where the system's relation problem
starts.

---

<a id="formal_session-239-original-4-15-marks-0d9b6dbb52"></a>
#### Original 4 · 15 marks

> **Question.** Critically examine whether the Sāṃkhya doctrine of many Puruṣas is proved or merely
> postulated.

**Model answer (277 words).**

Sāṃkhya argues for the plurality of witnesses rather than assuming it, but the arguments establish
less than the doctrine requires, so the honest verdict is "argued, not demonstrated".

The case rests on three arguments of *Sāṃkhyakārikā* 18. The fixed distribution of birth, death and
sense-faculties: one witness would mean one birth is every birth. The non-simultaneity of activity:
beings act at different times, whereas one self would make all streams act together. Differential
constitution by the qualities: temperaments differ as luminous, active or inert. A fourth
consideration, differential liberation, is a supplementary inference and not a phrase of that verse.

These arguments share a form: they infer transcendental plurality from empirical non-transferability.
That form is exactly what Advaita contests. Śaṃkara replies that every cited difference belongs to
limiting adjuncts (**upādhi**) — bodies, minds and quality-configurations — and that pure
consciousness, being without attribute, part, action or location, admits no principle of
individuation; Sāṃkhya therefore proves at most a plurality of mental streams (**citta**).

Sāṃkhya's counter is that bondage and release are non-transferable and that the reflection model
requires distinct loci in distinct intellects. This is a genuine reply, since Advaita must then treat
bondage itself as adventitious and the many empirical selves as apparent.

The residual difficulty is internal and should be conceded. If every witness is qualitatively
identical, the school cannot say what makes them numerically many; plurality is inferred from
non-transferable experience rather than grounded in a stated principle of individuation.

Critically examined, the doctrine is therefore well motivated and under-demonstrated: it is proved as
far as moral and experiential individuality can prove anything, and postulated precisely where
metaphysical individuation is demanded.

---

<a id="formal_session-240-original-5-20-marks-ad7957668d"></a>
#### Original 5 · 20 marks

> **Question.** Analyse the relation between Sāṃkhya's epistemology, its theory of causation and its
> account of liberation, and assess whether the three form a coherent system.

**Model answer (368 words).**

**The three parts.** Sāṃkhya's epistemology admits three instruments — perception, inference and
reliable testimony — and reduces all other candidates to them. Its causal doctrine holds that the
effect pre-exists in its material cause (**satkāryavāda**) in the specific form of real transformation
(**pariṇāmavāda**), effected by the modification of the three qualities. Its soteriology holds that
bondage is non-discrimination and liberation is discriminative knowledge issuing in isolation
(**kaivalya**).

**How epistemology drives causation.** Because both ultimates are beyond the senses, the entire
evidential burden falls on general-correlation inference. That is why the five proofs of primordial
nature exist at all — and the third of them, from operation due to causal power (**śaktitaḥ
pravṛtteś ca**), is already a causal thesis. Determinate production is the datum from which the
material root is inferred, and determinate production is what the doctrine of the pre-existent effect
explains. Epistemology and causation are therefore two descriptions of one inference.

**How causation drives soteriology.** Real transformation makes the world a genuine evolute rather
than an appearance, so bondage and release are events in a real order. But real transformation also
places intellect, I-maker and mind inside nature, which produces the reflection model: transparent
intellect receives the witness's reflection and false appropriation follows. Liberation must then be
the correction of an attribution rather than the destruction of an entity — which is exactly why
isolation is disentanglement rather than merger, and why the dancer image, not the prison image, is
canonical.

**How soteriology returns to epistemology.** The fifth proof of the witness — that striving for
release presupposes a liberable witness — makes the soteriology part of the evidence. The system
closes on itself.

**Assessment.** The coherence is genuine and unusually tight: each part supplies a premise the next
requires, and the enumeration of twenty-five principles is simultaneously a cosmology, a psychology
and the content of liberating knowledge.

The incoherence, where it appears, is concentrated at one joint. The disturbance of equilibrium is
attributed to mere presence, which is neither perception, nor inference from causal power, nor a real
transformation — the three things the system otherwise relies on.

Sāṃkhya is therefore coherent as a system of explanation and under-determined at its point of
origination, which is precisely where Śaṃkara concentrates his attack.

---

<a id="formal_session-241-original-6-20-marks-d30be5900f"></a>
#### Original 6 · 20 marks

> **Question.** "Sāṃkhya purchases the inactivity of consciousness at a high price." Discuss with
> reference to agency, moral responsibility and rebirth.

**Model answer (372 words).**

The statement is correct, and the price is best displayed by following one commitment through three
consequences.

**The commitment.** The conscious witness is pure, self-luminous, inactive and changeless
consciousness, wholly beyond the three qualities. Every productive function belongs to nature. This
is not an incidental thesis: it is what the proofs of the witness establish and what makes liberation
possible, since a really modified witness could never be restored.

**First consequence: agency.** Agency must be relocated entirely to nature's evolutes. The
determinative intellect ascertains, the I-maker appropriates, the mind coordinates — and all three are
insentient. What we experience as conscious agency is the two-way appearance: the insentient organ
appears conscious, and the inactive witness appears to be an agent. Nyāya objects that an agentless
self is otiose and makes its own self a substance with adventitious cognition, volition and effort.
Sāṃkhya's reply is uncompromising: ascribing agency to the witness is precisely the false
appropriation (**abhimāna**) in which bondage consists.

**Second consequence: moral responsibility.** If the witness never acts, it cannot straightforwardly
own a moral career. Sāṃkhya secures desert through the stable association of one subtle stream with
one numerically distinct witness. But this is asserted rather than derived, and it inherits the
school's deepest difficulty: qualitatively identical witnesses have no stated principle of
individuation, so the very individuality that moral ownership requires is the thing least well
grounded.

**Third consequence: rebirth.** Transmigration must be relocated too. The subtle body — eighteen
constituents: determinative intellect, I-maker, the eleven capacities including the mind, and the five
subtle elements — carries dispositions and karmic tendencies, conditioned by the intellect's eight
tendencies of merit and demerit, knowledge and ignorance, detachment and non-detachment, power and
powerlessness. The gross body from parental material falls away. The witness neither acts nor
literally migrates.

**Assessment.** The price is real but the purchase is deliberate. Inactivity buys a clean separation
of phenomenal consciousness from cognitive processing, an intelligible account of liberation as
correction rather than escape, and a non-theistic explanation of bondage. It costs an unowned agency,
an ungrounded principle of individuation and a rebirth without a traveller.

Whether the price is too high depends on the prior question the schools actually divide on: whether
consciousness may be described as doing anything at all.

<a id="formal_session-242-optional-advanced-depth-not-required-for-a-core-answer-692f438663"></a>
## OPTIONAL ADVANCED DEPTH — NOT REQUIRED FOR A CORE ANSWER

> **Status of this block.** Everything below is enrichment. No routed question in this topic requires
> it, and no answer in the practice sections above depends on it. The Basic Learning Session is
> independently answer-complete for every 10-, 15- and 20-mark demand recorded in the 2018–2025
> corpus. Use at most one optional move in a fifteen- or twenty-marker, and place it in the
> evaluative paragraph after the standard argument chain is complete. Two optional moves is the
> ceiling; three is padding.

<a id="formal_session-243-prerequisite-before-this-block-is-usable-3fcb403597"></a>
### Prerequisite before this block is usable

Master, from the Basic block, the following: one primordial material nature and many conscious
witnesses; the three qualities and their two modifications; the pre-existence of the effect in the
form of real transformation; the evolution of the twenty-five principles; the five proofs for the
witness; isolation as discriminative liberation; and Śaṃkara's critique as routed by the 2018–2025
questions. If any of these is shaky, the material below will make an answer look unfocused rather
than deep.

<a id="formal_session-244-advanced-module-1-what-exactly-does-proximity-mean-e8d449c270"></a>
### ADVANCED MODULE 1 — What exactly does "proximity" mean?

**The precise issue.** If the conscious witness and primordial nature are utterly different in kind,
and one of them is entirely inactive, what does their "proximity" actually amount to?

**Competing readings.** Summaries often slide into literal-contact language, which the system cannot
afford. A subtler reading takes the relation to be epistemic or reflective conjunction rather than
physical contact: what is "near" is not a body to a body but a light to a transparent medium.

**Strongest extra objection.** Without a real relation, the world-process never begins; a relation
that changes nothing in either relatum does no work.

**Counter-reply.** Sāṃkhya typically relies on presence-for-the-sake-of-experience rather than
mechanical interaction. The witness's efficacy is not productive causation but the mere presence that
renders nature's activity experienced. Critics nevertheless continue to find the relation
under-explained, and the honest advanced position is that this is a live difficulty rather than a
settled reply.

**Source trail.** Īśvarakṛṣṇa, *Sāṃkhya-kārikā*; Vācaspati Miśra, *Tattvakaumudī*; Gerald James
Larson, *Classical Sāṃkhya*.

**How to use it in one line.** "The relation is best read as reflective rather than physical
conjunction, which preserves inactivity at the cost of leaving the initiation of evolution
under-explained."

<a id="formal_session-245-advanced-module-2-can-teleology-survive-without-an-intelligent-principle-90704eb618"></a>
### ADVANCED MODULE 2 — Can teleology survive without an intelligent principle?

**The precise issue.** Can unconscious nature evolve "for" the witness's experience and liberation
without smuggling purpose in from elsewhere?

**Competing readings.** Sāṃkhya's internal teleology holds that nature serves the witness as a
structural fact of the quality-arrangement. Nyāya and Vedānta press the opposite direction: purposive
order points beyond unconscious matter to an intelligent orderer.

**Strongest extra objection.** Unconscious matter cannot pursue ends; "for the sake of" is either
literal, in which case intelligence is implied, or metaphorical, in which case it explains nothing.

**Counter-reply.** Sāṃkhya treats teleology as structural rather than intentional — milk flows for
the calf without deliberation — so "for the sake of" names a functional orientation, not an aim
entertained by anyone.

**Source trail.** Īśvarakṛṣṇa, *Sāṃkhya-kārikā*; S. Chatterjee and D. Datta, *An Introduction to
Indian Philosophy*; S. Radhakrishnan, *Indian Philosophy*, volume 2.

**How to use it in one line.** "Sāṃkhya's teleology is structural rather than intentional, which
answers the charge of covert theism but not the charge that structural purposiveness is a description
rather than an explanation."

<a id="formal_session-246-advanced-module-3-is-the-plurality-of-witnesses-demonstrated-or-defensiv-264f8fc32b"></a>
### ADVANCED MODULE 3 — Is the plurality of witnesses demonstrated or defensively postulated?

**The precise issue.** Are the arguments for many witnesses decisive, or partly introduced to
preserve individual bondage and liberation?

**Competing readings.** The classical defence holds that diversity of births, deaths and moral
trajectories implies plurality. Advaita presses that one consciousness can appear many through
limiting adjuncts, so the inference from empirical difference to transcendental plurality is
unlicensed.

**Strongest extra objection.** Plurality is inferentially weaker than Sāṃkhya claims, because every
cited difference is available to the Advaitin as a difference of adjunct.

**Counter-reply.** The school can answer that moral individuality and non-simultaneous liberation
become unintelligible on strict one-self models, so the postulate earns its place by what it
explains, even if it cannot be independently demonstrated.

**Source trail.** *Sāṃkhya-kārikā*; Vācaspati Miśra; C. D. Sharma, *A Critical Survey of Indian
Philosophy*.

**How to use it in one line.** "Plurality is best defended as an inference to the best explanation of
non-transferable bondage and release rather than as a demonstration from the nature of consciousness
itself."

<a id="formal_session-247-what-not-to-over-study-4c78732217"></a>
### What not to over-study

- Long textual-strata debates about every principle in the list.
- Specialist disputes on commentarial chronology, once the causation–proofs–liberation sequence is
  secure.
- The detailed later commentarial theism, exhaustive disposition cosmology and full Yoga practice
  taxonomies. These belong to other owners or to specialist study, not to a Sāṃkhya answer.

<a id="formal_session-248-when-this-block-would-stop-being-optional-b49f0f864e"></a>
### When this block would stop being optional

The advanced material would be promoted into core if a future question directly asked whether the
witness–nature relation is coherent, whether Sāṃkhya's teleology presupposes an intelligent
principle, or whether many witnesses are proved rather than postulated. Until then, the Basic block
carries every routed demand on its own.

<a id="formal_session-249-three-genuinely-contested-matters-to-report-as-contested-76e9bc586f"></a>
### Three genuinely contested matters to report as contested

- Whether early Sāṃkhya was originally theistic or consistently non-theistic remains debated.
- Whether isolation is purely negative aloneness or has a positive phenomenological character is
  disputed.
- The relation between the Kārikā tradition and the later Sūtra tradition is historically and
  doctrinally contested.

Report these as disputes. Presenting any one of them as settled is a factual error, not a stylistic
choice.

<a id="formal_session-250-consolidated-register-notes-e6103845eb"></a>
## CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

> **What these notes are.** Compressed revision material covering every subtopic taught above, in
> retrieval order rather than teaching order. They do not repeat the introductory prose of the
> sessions; they carry definitions, exact terminology, lists, comparisons, traps, answer routes and
> qualifications. The Basic/Optional-Advanced distinction is preserved: part N marks the enrichment
> boundary explicitly.

<a id="formal_session-251-a-system-identity-and-textual-control-6666ac4751"></a>
### A. System identity and textual control

- Classical Sāṃkhya = the system of **Īśvarakṛṣṇa's *Sāṃkhyakārikā***; commentaries: **Gauḍapāda**,
  **Vācaspati Miśra's *Tattvakaumudī***.
- **Kapila → Āsuri → Pañcaśikha**: traditional lineage; foundational works not extant in securely
  recoverable form. Name them; do not quote them.
- ***Sāṃkhyapravacana-sūtra* + Vijñānabhikṣu** = later, often more theistic phase. Never project
  back into the Kārikā system.
- The name is connected both with **enumeration** and with **discriminative or right knowledge** —
  both senses fit liberation through knowledge of twenty-five principles.
- Orthodox (**āstika**) school, but **non-theistic (nirīśvara)**. Write "non-theistic" or
  "God-unproved", not "atheistic".
- Syllabus item 16, verbatim printed wording: `Samkhya; Prakrit; Purusa; Causation; Liberation.`
- Yoga is a **bounded comparison**, never a source: it owns the mind-field, afflictions, eight limbs,
  meditative absorption and God as a special witness.

<a id="formal_session-252-b-threefold-suffering-and-the-shape-of-the-remedy-bda6d65054"></a>
### B. Threefold suffering and the shape of the remedy

- **duḥkha-traya**: internal or psychophysical (**ādhyātmika**) · from other beings and material
  surroundings (**ādhibhautika**) · from cosmic or unseen agencies (**ādhidaivika**).
- Visible remedies are **partial**; ritual results are **produced and therefore exhaustible**. Not
  false — graded.
- A final cure must **correct a mistake**, not manufacture a state ⇒ knowledge, not action.
- Knowledge needs content ⇒ **enumeration of twenty-five principles**.
- Register line: *the system enumerates because it promises release by discrimination.*

<a id="formal_session-253-c-instruments-of-valid-knowledge-and-the-inferential-burden-e359653965"></a>
### C. Instruments of valid knowledge and the inferential burden

| Item | Content |
|---|---|
| Admitted instruments | perception (**pratyakṣa**), inference (**anumāna**), reliable testimony (**āptavacana / śabda**) |
| Perception defined | ascertainment with respect to a presented object (**prativiṣayādhyavasāya**) through sense-capacities in contact |
| Inference, three kinds | **pūrvavat** (cause → effect) · **śeṣavat** (effect → cause) · **sāmānyato-dṛṣṭa** (general correlation, connection not itself perceived) |
| Standard example of the third | the sun seen in different positions ⇒ the sun moves |
| Testimony defined | word of a non-defective source, pre-eminently Veda/**śruti**, where the object is not otherwise easily known |
| Reductions | comparison → inference/testimony · postulation → inference · non-apprehension → perception's failure-conditions · inclusion → inference · traditional report → testimony when reliable |
| Causes of non-perception | excessive distance · excessive proximity · organ-defect · inattention · **subtlety (saukṣmya)** · obstruction · being overpowered · admixture with like things |
| Why the third kind is decisive | both ultimates are **beyond the senses (atīndriya)**; testimony begs the question against non-Vedic opponents |
| Presupposition | a general correlation of kinds can license inference to an entity of a kind never observed |
| Objectors | **Cārvāka** (invariable relation cannot be secured) · **Nyāya** (legitimate but over-extended; atoms + God is more economical) |

<a id="formal_session-254-d-cognition-reflection-and-apparent-agency-a27424cd32"></a>
### D. Cognition, reflection and apparent agency

- **Inner instrument (antaḥkaraṇa)** = intellect (**buddhi**) + I-maker (**ahaṃkāra**) + mind
  (**manas**).
- **Thirteenfold instrument (trayodaśa-karaṇa)** = 3 inner + 5 cognitive + 5 action capacities.
- Functions: intellect **determines** (final ascertainment, **adhyavasāya**) · I-maker
  **appropriates** ("I", "mine") · mind **coordinates**.
- External faculties = **doors (dvāra)**; inner organ = **doorkeeper (dvārin)**.
- Intellect is **sattva**-predominant ⇒ transparent ⇒ receives **buddhi-pratibimba /
  puruṣa-cchāyā-āpatti**.
- Two-way appearance: the **insentient (jaḍa)** organ appears conscious; the inactive witness appears
  to be an agent.
- Result: **false appropriation (abhimāna)** = the psychological root of bondage.
- Register line: *knowing is processing plus borrowed light.*

<a id="formal_session-255-e-primordial-material-nature-and-its-five-proofs-c03d59ef7d"></a>
### E. Primordial material nature and its five proofs

- **prakṛti** = unmanifest, unconscious, uncreated, primordial material principle; ultimate material
  cause.
- Names: **pradhāna** (chief) · **avyakta** (unmanifest, versus **vyakta**) · **mūla-prakṛti**
  (root).
- Exam-safe definition: the **unmanifest chief principle constituted by the three qualities**.
- Scope: "material" means **unconscious**, not physical. Intellect, ego, mind, capacities, subtle and
  gross elements are all inside it.
- Needed because a changeless, inactive witness cannot become a changing world, and Sāṃkhya's realism
  forbids reducing the world to appearance.

| # | Proof | Argues from | Delivers |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | **bhedānāṃ parimāṇāt** | finitude, delimitation, measurability | a subtler ground beneath limitation |
| 2 | **samanvayāt** | order and common pattern across effects | a common material basis |
| 3 | **śaktitaḥ pravṛtteś ca** | operation through causal power (**śakti**) | a productive power adequate to all effects |
| 4 | **kāraṇa-kārya-vibhāgāt** | cause and effect as distinguishable stages | an unmanifest causal matrix |
| 5 | **avibhāgāt vaiśvarūpyasya** ★ | the diversified cosmos is one interconnected whole | **nature must be ONE** |

- **Textual control:** *Sāṃkhyakārikā* 15 reads **śaktitaḥ pravṛtteś ca**, not *kāryataḥ pravṛtteḥ*
  — power, not planning.
- **2019 bridge sentence:** plurality at the root would destroy the undividedness from which the
  inference proceeds.

<a id="formal_session-256-f-the-three-qualities-and-their-two-modifications-868a4fa96e"></a>
### F. The three qualities and their two modifications

| Quality | Character | Affective tone | Function |
|---|---|---|---|
| **sattva** | lightness, illumination, buoyancy | pleasure | revelation, clarity, manifestation, knowledge |
| **rajas** | activity, restlessness, propulsion | pain | energy, stimulation, movement, mediation |
| **tamas** | heaviness, obstruction, inertia | delusion | concealment, resistance, fixation, density |

- Not three substances — three **inseparable constitutive tendencies** of one nature, present
  together in every evolute in varying proportion.
- **Equilibrium (sāmyāvasthā)** ⇒ nature is **unmanifest**; disturbed ⇒ manifest evolution.
- **sarūpa-pariṇāma** = homogeneous, each quality within its own nature, occurs in equilibrium.
- **virūpa-pariṇāma** = heterogeneous, across qualities, generates the manifest evolutes.
- Disturbance = **functional activation in proximity**, not mechanical collision.
- 2021 demand covers **both** the triad and the two modifications.

<a id="formal_session-257-g-the-conscious-witness-and-its-five-proofs-b705273a68"></a>
### G. The conscious witness and its five proofs

- **puruṣa** = pure consciousness, self-luminous, inactive, changeless, beyond the qualities.
  Consciousness is **essence, not quality**. Witness, seer, experiencer (**bhoktṛ**) — not producer.
- Never bound in its own nature; never productive.

| # | Proof | Core move |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | **saṅghāta-parārthatvāt** | aggregates are instrumental ⇒ they exist for a user distinct from them |
| 2 | **triguṇādi-viparyayāt** | the knower of pleasure, pain and delusion cannot be those same changing states |
| 3 | **adhiṣṭhānāt** | coordinated psychophysical life needs a presiding conscious locus — **not a God-proof** |
| 4 | **bhoktṛ-bhāvāt** | presented states require an experiencer distinct from them |
| 5 | **kaivalyārthaṃ pravṛtteḥ** | striving for release presupposes a liberable witness |

- Shared premise: everything objective is **unconscious, instrumental and presented**.
- Shared form: **sāmānyato-dṛṣṭa anumāna**.
- Evaluation: cumulative, not isolated. Honest limit — they yield a subject-pole, not yet an eternal,
  plural, wholly inactive one.

<a id="formal_session-258-h-plurality-of-witnesses-purposive-nature-and-the-empirical-self-1643050599"></a>
### H. Plurality of witnesses, purposive nature and the empirical self

- Plurality is **numerical, not qualitative**.
- ***Sāṃkhyakārikā* 18** supplies three arguments: fixed distribution of birth, death and faculties ·
  non-simultaneity of activity · differential constitution by the qualities.
- **Differential liberation is supplementary**, not a fourth phrase of the verse.
- Presuppositions: empirical individuation transfers upward · the witness is genuinely an experiencer
  · liberation is particular · numerical plurality without qualitative difference is intelligible.

| Objector | Objection | Reply | Residue |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Advaita** | differences are in limiting adjuncts (**upādhi**) ⇒ many mental streams (**citta**), not witnesses | bondage and release are non-transferable; reflection needs distinct loci | terminates in a clash of first principles |
| **Nyāya** | an inactive self cannot own moral desert | agency belongs to nature's evolutes; ascribing it is **abhimāna** | moral subject risks becoming empirical |
| **Buddhist** | a changeless actionless entity fails efficacy (**arthakriyā**) | efficacy is **mere presence (sannidhi)**, not production | non-productive efficacy may not be efficacy |
| **Internal** | what individuates qualitatively identical witnesses? | inferred from non-transferability of experience, bondage and release | the school's **weakest joint** |

- **Purposive nature (puruṣārtha)** — four images: **milk flowing for the calf** (unconscious
  functioning serves an end) · **lame and blind** (sees but cannot act / acts but cannot see) ·
  **the dancer who retires once seen** · **the modest woman who withdraws on being seen**.
- **Empirical self (jīva) is NOT a separate principle.** It is the witness as empirically conditioned
  through intellect, I-maker, mind, body and the karmic stream.

<a id="formal_session-259-i-the-twenty-five-principles-in-exact-order-f39ff68357"></a>
### I. The twenty-five principles in exact order

```text
prakṛti → mahat/buddhi → ahaṃkāra ─┬─ sāttvika line → manas + 5 jñānendriya + 5 karmendriya
                                   ├─ tāmasa line  → 5 tanmātra → 5 mahābhūta
                                   └─ rājasa factor → energises BOTH lines, produces NO new set
puruṣa = 25th principle, NOT an evolute
```

| No. | Principle |
|---|---|
| 1 | primordial nature (**prakṛti**) |
| 2 | the great one / determinative intellect (**mahat / buddhi**) — first evolute |
| 3 | I-maker (**ahaṃkāra**) |
| 4 | coordinating mind (**manas**) |
| 5–9 | five cognitive capacities: hearing, touch, sight, taste, smell |
| 10–14 | five action capacities: speech, grasping, locomotion, excretion, reproduction |
| 15–19 | five subtle elements: sound, touch, form, taste, smell potentials |
| 20–24 | five gross elements: ether, air, fire, water, earth |
| 25 | conscious witness (**puruṣa**) — non-evolute |

- **Fourfold classification:** 1 cause-not-effect (nature) · 7 both (intellect, I-maker, five subtle
  elements) · 16 effects-only (mind, ten capacities, five gross elements) · 1 neither (witness).
- **Correspondence:** sound → ether · touch → air · form → fire · taste → water · smell → earth.
- Do not import Advaita's fivefold grossification (**pañcīkaraṇa**).
- **mahat = buddhi**, one principle under a cosmic and an individual name. The I-maker derives from
  it.

<a id="formal_session-260-j-subtle-body-transmigration-and-karma-d063741f6e"></a>
### J. Subtle body, transmigration and karma

- **liṅga-śarīra / sūkṣma-śarīra** = the continuing psychophysical vehicle between gross
  embodiments.
- Count: **18** = intellect (1) + I-maker (1) + eleven capacities including the mind (11) + five
  subtle elements (5).
- Conditioned by dispositions (**bhāva**) and the **eight tendencies of intellect**: merit/demerit ·
  knowledge/ignorance · detachment/non-detachment · power/powerlessness.
- At death the **gross body from parental material** falls away; the subtle body carries dispositions
  and karmic tendencies onward.
- **The witness neither acts nor literally migrates.** Transmigration belongs to the subtle body and
  the empirical self-complex operating for a particular witness.
- Pressure point: moral ownership rests on the **stable association** of one subtle stream with one
  numerically distinct witness — asserted, not grounded.

<a id="formal_session-261-k-the-pre-existent-effect-and-the-five-arguments-1b1c425363"></a>
### K. The pre-existent effect and the five arguments

- **satkāryavāda** = the effect pre-exists in its material cause; production is **manifestation
  (abhivyakti)**, not creation from non-being.

| # | Argument | Standing example | Counter | Reply |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **asadakaraṇāt** | no hare's horn; no oil from sand | Nyāya: equivocation between absolutely and not-yet non-existent | the not-yet effect must still be something determinate |
| 2 | **upādāna-grahaṇāt** | milk for curd; threads for cloth; mango seed not sand | restriction is explained by capacity | determinate capacity **is** latent presence |
| 3 | **sarvasambhavābhāvāt** | curd from milk, not from water | restriction comes from the constitution of causes | a constitution capable of only X **is** X latently |
| 4 | **śaktasya śakya-karaṇāt** | milk has potency for curd, not cloth | Nyāya: potency is relational | relational potency cannot explain determinacy |
| 5 | **kāraṇa-bhāvāt** | cloth is threads arranged; ornament and gold | Nyāya: the whole (**avayavin**) is a new substance | new properties are new arrangements (**saṃsthāna**) |

- **Mango-seed formula:** latent effect + material continuity + real transformation. **Never** a
  miniature gross tree inside the seed.

<a id="formal_session-262-l-real-transformation-against-the-rival-causal-theories-ba97c78902"></a>
### L. Real transformation against the rival causal theories

| Position | School | Core claim | Cause | World |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **pariṇāmavāda** | Sāṃkhya | transformation is real | primordial nature | real evolute |
| **vivartavāda** | Advaita | transformation is apparent | Brahman | ultimately appearance |
| **asatkāryavāda / ārambhavāda** | Nyāya-Vaiśeṣika | effect absent beforehand (**prāgabhāva**); production is a new beginning | material + efficient causes | a fresh product |
| **pratītyasamutpāda** | Buddhism | dependent arising without an enduring material root | conditions | a conditioned series |

- **Shared premise with Advaita:** no being from non-being ⇒ both fall under a broad pre-existence
  orientation. **Decisive difference:** **pariṇāma** versus **vivarta**.
- Three standing objections and replies: effort pointless → **manifestation**; cause/effect collapse →
  difference of **state (avasthā)**; world always as now → only the **capacity** is latent.
- Advaita reaction (2018): unconscious cause cannot be the scriptural cause · a really changing
  ultimate is mutable and dependent · superimposition explains dependence better.
- Sāṃkhya defence (2018): production is **selective** and only material continuity explains it ·
  mutability disqualifies only a *changeless* ultimate · appearance-theory buys immutability at the
  cost of the reality of bondage and release.

<a id="formal_session-263-m-bondage-discriminative-knowledge-isolation-and-non-theism-0c92a28dcd"></a>
### M. Bondage, discriminative knowledge, isolation and non-theism

- **Bondage = aviveka**, non-discrimination; generated by reflection and stabilised as
  **abhimāna**. Not a real alteration in the witness.
- **Liberation = viveka-jñāna**, the steady discrimination "I am not nature".
- **State = kaivalya**, isolation or aloneness. **Disentanglement, not merger, not annihilation.**
- **jīvanmukti** = liberation while the body continues by prior momentum — **potter's wheel**.
  **videhamukti** = final cessation of embodiment.
- Strict formulation: the witness is **never really bound, liberated or transmigrating**; knowledge
  ends false attribution.
- **Goal names must not be interchanged:** isolation (Sāṃkhya) ≠ identity with Brahman (Advaita) ≠
  cessation of suffering (Nyāya).
- **Five non-theistic arguments:** (1) a God acting from desire is neither perfect nor free; (2) an
  eternally fulfilled God has no motive to create a painful world; (3) distributing results under
  karma limits divine independence, arbitrary distribution destroys justice; (4) an immutable
  conscious God cannot be the material source of a changing unconscious world under Sāṃkhya's
  cause-effect continuity; (5) eternal witnesses plus primordial nature already explain consciousness
  and material evolution.
- Boundary caution: later theistic readings (Vijñānabhikṣu) must not be projected backwards; Yoga
  adds God as a special witness and owns the practical discipline.

<a id="formal_session-264-n-the-contact-problem-śaṃkara-s-critique-the-answer-route-and-the-verdic-fc5c2ddf71"></a>
### N. The contact problem, Śaṃkara's critique, the answer route and the verdict

- **Problem:** an inactive witness and an unconscious nature can stand in no obvious real relation,
  yet evolution, bondage and liberation all presuppose one.
- **Sāṃkhya answer:** **mere proximity (sānnidhya)**, not gross contact; nature becomes active in the
  witness's mere presence.
- **Two analogies, two jobs:** **magnet and iron** = activation by presence; **crystal and flower** =
  apparent contamination without real change.
- **Śaṃkara's attack on the analogies:** a magnet **exerts force**, so the witness would not be wholly
  inactive; the crystal explains appearance but **not initiation**.
- **Why chief wrestler (pradhāna malla):** (1) most sophisticated non-Vedāntic rival at the level of
  ultimate explanation; (2) real transformation is the nearest rival to apparent transformation;
  (3) both appeal to a single ultimate explanatory ground.
- **Four objections:** unconscious nature cannot be the Upaniṣadic cause · the contact problem is
  unresolved · teleology cannot rest on unconscious matter · knowing-cause texts exclude an
  insentient nature.
- **Pressed verdict:** the dualism is unstable — it collapses toward monism or needs a third
  mediating principle.

**Four standing criticisms with replies.**

| Criticism | Reply | Residual force |
|---|---|---|
| teleology without intelligence | purposive functioning need not be conscious planning | vulnerable to Vedāntic pressure |
| relation between two absolutes | proximity, reflection, false attribution — not fusion | partly analogical; the weakest point |
| plurality of witnesses is unnecessary | non-simultaneous liberation and individual experience | the limiting-adjunct reply is unanswered |
| the pre-existent effect removes novelty | manifestation from latent to patent | Nyāya presses the new-whole objection |

**Debate axes for cross-school answers.**

| Axis | Sāṃkhya | Contrast |
|---|---|---|
| Self | many inactive witnesses | Advaita: one self · Nyāya: many active selves · Buddhism: no permanent self |
| Causation | pre-existent effect + real transformation | Nyāya: prior non-existence · Advaita: apparent transformation |
| World-process | rooted in enduring nature | Buddhism: dependent origination |
| God | non-theistic | Yoga, Nyāya, Vedānta: explicit God or Brahman |
| Liberation | isolation | Advaita: identity · Nyāya: cessation of suffering |

**PYQ answer route, 2018–2025.**

| Year · Q | Marks | Route |
|---|---:|---|
| 2018 Q5(e) | 10 | Kārikā 18 three arguments → supplementary liberation argument → Advaita adjunct objection → non-transferability reply → concede individuation |
| 2018 Q7(b) | 15 | real transformation stated → three-part Advaita reaction → three-part Sāṃkhya defence → shared premise → clash of first principles |
| 2019 Q5(d) | 10 | witness defined → empirical self is not a principle → reflection mechanism → bondage as misattribution → critical limit |
| 2019 Q8(c) | 15 | name **avibhāgāt vaiśvarūpyasya** → show the other four permit plurality → bridge sentence → Nyāya's refusal |
| 2020 Q7(a) | 20 | latent-effect formula → five arguments → reject Nyāya, Advaita and Buddhism by name → manifestation reply |
| 2021 Q6(b) | 15 | triad with character, tone, function → equilibrium → homogeneous and heterogeneous modification → trigger problem |
| 2022 Q5(a) | 10 | five proofs → shared premise and form → cumulative evaluation → honest limit |
| 2023 Q5(b) | 10 | quoted relation problem → proximity reply → two analogies → Śaṃkara on both → instability verdict |
| 2024 Q7(a) | 20 | full evolution tree and fourfold classification → **mahat = buddhi** → three functional roles |
| 2025 Q7(a) | 20 | three grounds for the title → four objections examined → verdict on what the critique establishes |
| 2021 Q5(a) routed | 10 | Nyāya "no" via prior non-existence → Sāṃkhya "yes" via latent capacity → equivocation charge → first-principles verdict |
| 2022 Q5(e) routed | 10 | partial agreement → shared denial of production from non-being → opposite commitments → question-begging verdict |
| 2023 Q8(b) routed | 15 | prior non-existence defined → its defensive work → the dilemma against Sāṃkhya → Sāṃkhya's three replies → relocation verdict |

**Trap ledger — twenty-four checkable errors.**

1. Calling the threefold suffering a printed syllabus term.
2. Saying ritual means are false rather than exhaustible.
3. Citing Vijñānabhikṣu as evidence about the classical system.
4. Writing "atheistic" without the non-theistic qualification.
5. Giving Sāṃkhya four or six instruments of knowledge.
6. Saying Sāṃkhya *rejects* comparison or postulation instead of reducing them.
7. Treating imperceptibility as a refutation instead of invoking subtlety.
8. Calling general-correlation inference "vague inference".
9. Saying the mind determines or the intellect coordinates.
10. Describing reflection as a real modification of the witness.
11. Equating nature with matter as opposed to mind.
12. Naming proof 1 or 2 as the one-nature proof.
13. Reading *Sāṃkhyakārikā* 15 as *kāryataḥ pravṛtteḥ*, turning power into purpose.
14. Calling the three qualities three substances.
15. Stopping at the triad in a question that asks for modifications too.
16. Presenting the third proof of the witness as a God-proof.
17. Making consciousness a property of the witness.
18. Citing differential liberation as a fourth phrase of *Sāṃkhyakārikā* 18.
19. Treating the empirical self as a twenty-sixth principle.
20. Splitting the great one and the determinative intellect into two stages.
21. Giving the active quality its own product-set.
22. Counting the subtle body as seventeen by dropping the mind.
23. Defending the mango seed as containing a miniature gross tree.
24. Interchanging isolation, identity with Brahman and cessation of suffering.

**Graded verdicts, ready to adapt.**

- **Strong:** rigorously argued non-theistic realism; economical theory of qualitative variety; a
  causal thesis that explains determinate production and material continuity; an early separation of
  phenomenal consciousness from cognitive processing.
- **Weak:** the witness–nature relation is under-explained; unconscious teleology is difficult;
  individuation of attributeless witnesses is the weakest joint; the beginning of bondage is obscure.
- **Contested:** whether early Sāṃkhya was theistic; whether isolation is purely negative; how the
  Kārikā and Sūtra traditions relate.
- **10 marks:** one nature explains the world, many witnesses explain experience, discrimination
  explains liberation.
- **15 marks:** the power lies in the mutual fit of instruments, causation, qualities and isolation,
  though the contact problem invites Vedāntic criticism.
- **20 marks:** a philosophically serious non-theistic system whose causal realism and psychology of
  reflection are impressive, but whose unconscious teleology and plurality of attributeless witnesses
  remain unresolved.

**Optional-Advanced boundary.** Nothing in parts A–N above depends on the Optional Advanced block.
That block adds only three enrichment moves — the precise meaning of proximity, whether teleology
survives without an intelligent principle, and whether plurality is demonstrated or postulated — each
usable as a single sentence in an evaluative paragraph.

<a id="formal_session-266-ascii-master-flow-panel-1-14-the-controlling-question-and-the-shape-of-t-e1c13b37d4"></a>
#### ASCII MASTER FLOW — PANEL 1/14: The controlling question and the shape of the remedy

```ascii-master
ASCII MASTER FLOW — PANEL 1/14: The controlling question and the shape of the remedy

  SYLLABUS ITEM 16, PRINTED VERBATIM: "Samkhya; Prakrit; Purusa; Causation; Liberation."
  normalised: Sāṃkhya · prakṛti · puruṣa · causation · liberation
                                  |
  CONTROLLING QUESTION ...... how can suffering be ended FINALLY and NON-RETURNINGLY?
                                  |
  +---- THREEFOLD SUFFERING (duḥkha-traya) — the exhaustive diagnosis ------------------+
  |  ādhyātmika    internal / psychophysical .... bodily disease, mental affliction     |
  |  ādhibhautika  from other beings + surroundings ... humans, animals, environment    |
  |  ādhidaivika   from cosmic / elemental / unseen agencies (traditional class)        |
  +------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
                                  |
  WHY ORDINARY EXITS FAIL
     visible remedies (medicine, wealth, protection) -> relief is PARTIAL and decays
     ritual / scriptural means -> the result is PRODUCED -> perishable -> repeatable
     => GRADED AS EXHAUSTIBLE, NOT DENOUNCED AS FALSE   [trap 2]
                                  |
  THEREFORE the required remedy must be NON-PRODUCTIVE
     it cannot manufacture a state; it must CORRECT A MISTAKE
     correcting a mistake = KNOWLEDGE, not action
                                  |
  THEREFORE the knowledge must have CONTENT
     "Sāṃkhya" is connected BOTH with ENUMERATION and with DISCRIMINATIVE/RIGHT KNOWLEDGE
     => the content is the TWENTY-FIVE PRINCIPLES (tattva)
                                  |
  +------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
  |  ANSWER-GRABBING SPINE                                                              |
  |  Sāṃkhya is a soteriology forced to become a cosmology: a system promising release  |
  |  by knowledge must first specify, exhaustively, what there is to be known.          |
  +------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

  ! TRAP 1: do not present duḥkha-traya as a printed syllabus noun. It is the doctrinal
    starting point that explains why the printed clause ends with "Liberation".
```

<a id="formal_session-267-ascii-master-flow-panel-2-14-system-identity-and-the-textual-ladder-1f0856c503"></a>
#### ASCII MASTER FLOW — PANEL 2/14: System identity and the textual ladder

```ascii-master
ASCII MASTER FLOW — PANEL 2/14: System identity and the textual ladder

  RUNG                          WHAT IT IS                        WHAT IT LICENSES
  ---------------------------   -------------------------------   ------------------------------
  Kapila -> Āsuri -> Pañcaśikha traditional founders; works NOT    name the lineage;
                                extant in securely recoverable     DO NOT quote them
                                form
  ---------------------------   -------------------------------   ------------------------------
  ĪŚVARAKṚṢṆA,                  earliest EXTANT systematic         THIS IS "SĀṂKHYA"
  SĀṂKHYAKĀRIKĀ           *     classical text                     unless told otherwise
  ---------------------------   -------------------------------   ------------------------------
  Gauḍapāda's commentary;       major explanatory traditions       supporting commentarial
  Vācaspati Miśra,              on the Kārikā                      authority
  TATTVAKAUMUDĪ
  ---------------------------   -------------------------------   ------------------------------
  Sāṃkhyapravacana-sūtra;       later textual phase, often more    cite ONLY as a labelled later
  Vijñānabhikṣu           !     theistic and reconciliatory        development; NEVER project it
                                                                   back into the Kārikā system

  DOCTRINAL IDENTITY OF THE CLASSICAL SYSTEM
     realist DUALISM: one unconscious prakṛti + many inactive conscious puruṣas
     causation: satkāryavāda in the specific form of pariṇāmavāda
     soteriology: kaivalya by viveka-jñāna
     theology: NIRĪŚVARA — non-theistic / God-unproved, NOT "atheistic by proof"   [trap 4]
     status: ORTHODOX (āstika) school that nevertheless requires no creator

  BOUNDARY WITH YOGA (bounded comparison, never a source)
     Sāṃkhya supplies ....... the dualist metaphysics Yoga substantially shares
     Yoga owns .............. mind-field, afflictions, eight limbs, meditative absorption,
                              God as a special conscious witness, the yogi's perception
     => Sāṃkhya = non-theistic and theoretical | Yoga = theistic and practical

  ANSWER LINE: chronological hygiene is a philosophical commitment — a later theistic
  commentary cannot soften a non-theism the classical system deliberately argues for.
```

<a id="formal_session-268-ascii-master-flow-panel-3-14-instruments-of-valid-knowledge-and-the-infe-d494844982"></a>
#### ASCII MASTER FLOW — PANEL 3/14: Instruments of valid knowledge and the inferential burden

```ascii-master
ASCII MASTER FLOW — PANEL 3/14: Instruments of valid knowledge and the inferential burden

  ADMITTED — EXACTLY THREE                    REDUCED — absorbed, NOT rejected   [trap 6]
  ----------------------------------------    --------------------------------------------
  1 PERCEPTION (pratyakṣa)                    upamāna      -> inference / testimony
    prativiṣayādhyavasāya: ascertainment      arthāpatti   -> inference
    with respect to a presented object        anupalabdhi  -> perception's FAILURE-CONDITIONS
    through sense-capacities in contact       sambhava     -> inference
                                              aitihya      -> testimony when reliable
  2 INFERENCE (anumāna) — THREE KINDS
    pūrvavat ......... antecedent/cause -> effect ....... clouds -> rain
    śeṣavat .......... effect/residue -> cause .......... one drop -> the sea is salty
    sāmānyato-dṛṣṭa .. general correlation; the connection between the terms is
                       NOT ITSELF PERCEIVED ............. sun in different positions -> it moves

  3 RELIABLE TESTIMONY (āptavacana / śabda)
    word of a non-defective source, pre-eminently Veda/śruti, where the object is not
    otherwise easily known

  EIGHT CAUSES OF NON-PERCEPTION
    excessive distance · excessive proximity · organ-defect · inattention of mind ·
    SUBTLETY (saukṣmya) · obstruction by an intervening object · being overpowered ·
    admixture with like things
    => mūla-prakṛti's imperceptibility is assigned to SUBTLETY; puruṣa is likewise
      atīndriya rather than non-existent.  IMPERCEPTIBILITY IS NOT ABSENCE   [trap 7]

  +---------------------------- THE SELF-IMPOSED DEBT ---------------------------------+
  | both ultimates are BEYOND THE SENSES (atīndriya) -> perception cannot reach them   |
  | testimony alone BEGS THE QUESTION against non-Vedic opponents                      |
  | => THE WHOLE EVIDENTIAL WEIGHT FALLS ON SĀMĀNYATO-DṚṢṬA INFERENCE                   |
  | => the five proofs of prakṛti and the five proofs of puruṣa ARE that payment        |
  +------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

  PRESUPPOSITION: a general correlation of KINDS can license inference to an entity of a
  kind never itself observed as a sense-object.
  OBJ Cārvāka: vyāpti cannot be secured for a term never perceived.
      REPLY: strict perceptualism cannot explain ordinary causal reasoning either.
  OBJ Nyāya: legitimate but over-extended — atoms + Īśvara is more economical.
      REPLY: adding God does not explain the MATERIAL CONTINUITY between cause and effect.
```

  ----------------------------------     -----------------------------------------------------

    (jñānendriya / buddhīndriya)              |
    hearing · touch · sight ·                 v

                                              |

    (karmendriya)                        BUDDHI ..... FINAL ASCERTAINMENT (adhyavasāya):
    speech · grasping · locomotion ·                  judgement and discrimination
    excretion · reproduction                          sattva-predominant => TRANSPARENT

  5 + 5 + 3 = THIRTEENFOLD INSTRUMENT (trayodaśa-karaṇa)
  ! a functional grouping drawn from within the 25 — NOT thirteen extra principles

                    PURUṢA — pure light, never acts
                             |
                             v  reflection falls into transparent buddhi
             +--------------------------------------------------+
             | puruṣa-cchāyā-āpatti / buddhi-pratibimba          |
             +--------------------------------------------------+
                             |
              +--------------+---------------+
              v                              v
   THE INSENTIENT (jaḍa) ORGAN     THE INACTIVE WITNESS
   APPEARS CONSCIOUS               APPEARS TO BE AN AGENT
              +--------------+---------------+
                             v
              FALSE APPROPRIATION (abhimāna)
              "I am the doer / knower / enjoyer"
              = THE PSYCHOLOGICAL ROOT OF BONDAGE

  ORDERING TO KEEP EXACT: reflection is a STRUCTURAL FACT -> appropriation is the ERROR
  -> bondage is the CONSEQUENCE.
  ! TRAP 10: reflection must NOT be described as a real modification of puruṣa; a really
    modified changeless principle could never be released.
  ANSWER LINE: the intellect processes without awareness, the witness is aware without
  processing, and a conscious thought is the borrowed light of the second on the first.
